    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	ThreadGate modulates the residual stream with per-layer gated MLP networks and an auxiliary GAR loss. Training succeeds (TF GAR\,=\,0.50), but autoregressive GAR collapses 20--33$\times$ (exposure bias), confirmed on Llama-3.1-8B (12.5$\times$). Inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5, GAR 0.727) and scheduled sampling (gap 32$\times$$\to$1.57$\times$, no PPL cost, single seed) both demonstrate attention misallocation---not representation degradation---as the proximal cause.
4	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0144GxJMCaopTqZXqT9QDHaa`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	
6	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}). The combined loss $\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\mathcal{L}_\text{div}$ uses $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\tau = 0.5$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.
7	
8	We evaluate five configurations that form a factorial design isolating four causal factors. \textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \S\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.
9	
10	\subsection{Main Results}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that, under the single seed tested, all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.
13	
14	The Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
15	
16	The teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
17	
18	\begin{table*}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. IID evaluation uses teacher-forced UltraChat text; held-out uses 50 non-overlapping conversations. All four fine-tuned variants converge to within 0.75\,pp on GAR-ours; PPL is indistinguishable. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}.}
22	\label{tab:four-way}
23	\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}
24	\toprule
25	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{IID Evaluation}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Held-out Evaluation}} \\
26	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
27	\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL \\
28	\midrule
29	Base              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 \\
30	ThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 \\
31	\;+\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
32	Uniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
33	LoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}
36	\end{table*}
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	  \centering
40	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
41	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\,=\,45, $\pm$1\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 with 7--8\% decay (T1$\to$T4); Base decays 80\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}
42	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
46	
47	The preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.
48	
49	Figure~\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\,=\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\beta \in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\,$\to$\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,5: GAR increases ${\sim}$11$\times$ (0.062\,$\to$\,0.6795$\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\% (1.41\,$\to$\,1.91). At $\beta$\,=\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.
50	
51	$N$\,=\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\,=\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\pm$0.110 ($\beta$\,=\,2) to 0.556$\pm$0.060 ($\beta$\,=\,3), an 8.7$\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\% PPL increase (1.43\,$\to$\,1.56), identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\beta$\,=\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\,=\,5 evaluation (0.028).
52	
53	These results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.
54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	  \centering
57	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
58	  \caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\pm$1\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\,=\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\beta \in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\sim}$11$\times$ recovery with 35\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\,=\,50, 39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,3, identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\times$ GAR recovery, 9\% PPL cost). High-$\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}
59	  \label{fig:steering}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62

Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.\n\nWe evaluate five configurations that form a factorial design isolating four causal factors. \\textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \\textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \\citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \\textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \\textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\\mathcal{L}_\\text{NTP} + \\mathcal{L}_\\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \\textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \\emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \\emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \\S\\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that, under the single seed tested, all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. IID evaluation uses teacher-forced UltraChat text; held-out uses 50 non-overlapping conversations. All four fine-tuned variants converge to within 0.75\\,pp on GAR-ours; PPL is indistinguishable. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID Evaluation}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out Evaluation}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nThe preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\\beta \\in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\\,$\\to$\\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028).\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",

```text
1	\section{Analysis}\label{sec:analysis}
2	
3	The five-way comparison in \S\ref{sec:experiments} established that gating produces no measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning. This section traces the failure through a three-step causal chain: we first show that gates learn behavior opposite to the design intent (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}), then rule out gradient conflict as the explanation through a causal exclusion experiment (\S\ref{subsec:pcgrad}), and finally identify distribution shift as the root cause (\S\ref{subsec:dist-shift}). \S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity} confirms this selectivity with an orthogonal hidden-state metric, and \S\ref{subsec:cross-model} confirms the failure generalizes across architecturally distinct LLMs.
4	
5	\subsection{Gate Turn Conditioning}\label{subsec:gate-conditioning}
6	
7	The design intent of ThreadGate is to modulate the residual stream more aggressively at late conversation turns, where GAR naturally decays (\S\ref{sec:background}). We test whether the learned gates achieve this by comparing gate values at turn~1 (early) and turn~21 (late) across all 28 layers of the trained model.
8	
9	The gates do the opposite. In 27 of 28 layers, the gate value at turn~1 is lower than at turn~21 (Wilcoxon signed-rank test: $p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$, effect size $r = 0.975$). At layer~23, the gate is 0.769 at turn~1 and 0.979 at turn~21; at layer~14, it is 0.747 at turn~1 and 0.928 at turn~21. The pattern is consistent: gates suppress the residual stream more at early turns (where GAR is already high) and become nearly passive at late turns (where intervention is most needed). This inversion has a straightforward explanation. NTP loss pressure is highest at late turns, where the model must predict tokens conditioned on a long context. The NTP gradient pushes gates toward 1.0 to preserve the full sublayer computation, and this pressure is strongest precisely where GAR-based attenuation would be most useful.
10	
11	The resulting inference-time gate values range from 0.93 to 0.98 (2--7\% attenuation), far below what meaningful modulation would require; this explains the convergence with LoRA-only in Table~\ref{tab:four-way}. Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}(a) visualizes the per-layer gate difference. The inverted turn conditioning raises a natural question: does the NTP-GAR gradient conflict prevent gates from learning the intended behavior? If gradient conflict is the root cause, resolving it should rescue gate conditioning.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	  \centering
15	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_gate_shift.pdf}
16	  \caption{(a)~Gate turn conditioning: per-layer gate values at turn~1 vs turn~21. In 27/28 layers, gates approach 1.0 at late turns, inverting the design intent ($p = 3.73 \times 10^{-8}$). (b)~Distribution shift: the 20--33$\times$ GAR gap persists across all five variants (TG, +PCGrad, UG, LoRA-only, Base), the failure is universal across all configurations.}
17	  \label{fig:gate-shift}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\subsection{PCGrad Causal Exclusion}\label{subsec:pcgrad}
21	
22	The NTP and GAR gradients point in opposing directions across 68--79\% of layers throughout training, with mean cosine similarity ranging from $-$0.089 (epoch~0) to $-$0.042 (epoch~2). The NTP gradient magnitude dominates by 7.5--14.7$\times$. This raises a natural hypothesis: perhaps opposing gradient directions prevent the GAR objective from converging. If so, eliminating direction conflict should prevent the collapse. We test this using PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, which projects the gradient of one task onto the normal plane of the other whenever two task gradients conflict. We apply PCGrad to gate parameters only, scoping our claim to direction conflict; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibrium violations \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested.
23	
24	Eliminating direction conflict via PCGrad shifts per-step cosine similarity from consistently negative ($-$0.089) to oscillating around zero ($-$0.15 to $+$0.09), yet all outcomes remain unchanged: IID GAR-ours (0.50 vs 0.50), PPL (2.780 vs 2.784), and autoregressive GAR (0.015 vs 0.016) are indistinguishable (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). $\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ follows a nearly identical training trajectory and collapses in the same manner (Figure~\ref{fig:training-pcgrad}b). The causal exclusion follows by \emph{modus tollens}: if direction conflict causes the failure, removing it should prevent it; PCGrad removes it; the failure persists; therefore direction conflict is not the cause. Direction conflict is neither necessary (removing it does not rescue GAR) nor sufficient ($\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ converges during training despite persistent conflict). The 7.5--14.7$\times$ NTP magnitude dominance renders directional interference inconsequential. With gradient conflict ruled out, the question narrows: the training dynamics are not the problem (GAR converges under teacher forcing), so the failure must occur at the transition from training to inference.
25	
26	\begin{figure}[t]
27	  \centering
28	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_training_pcgrad.pdf}
29	  \caption{(a)~Gradient cosine similarity between NTP and GAR objectives. ThreadGate remains consistently negative; +PCGrad oscillates around zero. (b)~IID evaluation outcomes are indistinguishable: eliminating direction conflict changes nothing.}
30	  \label{fig:training-pcgrad}
31	\end{figure}
32	
33	\subsection{Distribution Shift Analysis}\label{subsec:dist-shift}
34	
35	Gates are ineffective because NTP pressure pushes them toward identity (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}), and this is not caused by gradient direction conflict (\S\ref{subsec:pcgrad}). The remaining question: why do GAR improvements under teacher forcing fail to transfer to autoregressive generation?
36	
37	The 20--33$\times$ GAR gap (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}) persists across all training strategies: the model learns the intended attention pattern on the training distribution but collapses under model-generated text. GAR-Dongre provides a revealing control: under standard teacher-forced training it remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) with no distribution gap, so the shift selectively destroys the concentrated last-token attention pattern while leaving diffuse attention unaffected (scheduled sampling breaks this pattern; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
38	
39	The mechanism is exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled}: during training, the model conditions on ground-truth prefixes; at inference, on its own outputs. The statistical properties diverge, and the 2--7\% gate attenuation (\S\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) cannot compensate. \citet{he2019exposure} showed that language models exhibit token-level self-recovery from exposure bias, and our PPL results confirm this (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}). The critical distinction is between primary and auxiliary objectives: NTP is robust because it is the same task at both training and inference time, while GAR optimizes a distributional property of the input prefix that has no counterpart under autoregressive generation. Figure~\ref{fig:attention-heatmap} confirms teacher-forced GAR-ours remains near $\tau = 0.5$ (7--8\% decay, $N$\,=\,45), yet autoregressive GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).
40	
41	\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}
42	
43	\S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours (20--33$\times$ gap) while, under standard training, GAR-Dongre remains unaffected. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with an orthogonal hidden-state probe.
44	
45	We define Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC) as the cosine similarity between hidden states at the last-token position of adjacent assistant turns. If exposure bias caused broad representational failure, TRC should show a comparable gap. It does not. For ThreadGate, teacher-forced TRC is 0.334 and autoregressive TRC is 0.332 (gap $= +0.002$). For LoRA-only, teacher-forced TRC is 0.349 and autoregressive TRC is 0.411 (gap $= -0.062$, reversed): autoregressive generation does not degrade hidden-state coherence. Neither checkpoint shows a TRC gap comparable to the 20--33$\times$ GAR collapse.
46	
47	The slightly elevated autoregressive TRC for LoRA-only likely reflects higher token-level predictability in model-generated text, which produces more self-similar hidden states. These results confirm the selectivity from \S\ref{subsec:dist-shift} with an orthogonal metric: exposure bias disrupts a specific learned routing behavior (summary-position attention to goal tokens) while leaving internal representations intact.
48	
49	Cross-model evaluation extends this selectivity: GAR-Dongre shows negligible distribution shift on both Llama-3.1-8B (AR/TF ratio 1.002$\times$) and Qwen-2.5-7B (1.28$\times$), compared to the 12.5--33$\times$ collapse in GAR-ours, confirming that exposure bias primarily disrupts attention concentration at generation boundaries rather than aggregate attention patterns (the scheduled sampling experiment in \S\ref{sec:discussion} breaks this pattern, with GAR-Dongre rising to 0.353).
50	
51	\subsection{Cross-Model Generality}\label{subsec:cross-model}
52	
53	To test whether the exposure bias failure generalizes, we replicate the full evaluation pipeline on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \citep{grattafiori2024llama3}. Phase~1 diagnostics confirm goal attention decay (GAR: 0.026$\to$0.003 over 10 turns, 90.1\% decline vs.\ Qwen's 84.7\%). A matched evaluation ($N$\,=\,50, $T$\,=\,4) quantifies the exposure bias gap: teacher-forced GAR-ours is 0.484, autoregressive is 0.039, a 12.5$\times$ gap directionally consistent with Qwen's 20--33$\times$. The gate profile differs---Qwen's minimum at layers~3--4, Llama's at layer~25---yet both converge toward identity under NTP pressure. The exposure bias gap is consistent across architecturally distinct LLMs.
```

The statistical properties diverge, and the 2--7\\% gate attenuation (\\S\\ref{subsec:gate-conditioning}) cannot compensate. \\citet{he2019exposure} showed that language models exhibit token-level self-recovery from exposure bias, and our PPL results confirm this (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}). The critical distinction is between primary and auxiliary objectives: NTP is robust because it is the same task at both training and inference time, while GAR optimizes a distributional property of the input prefix that has no counterpart under autoregressive generation. Figure~\\ref{fig:attention-heatmap} confirms teacher-forced GAR-ours remains near $\\tau = 0.5$ (7--8\\% decay, $N$\\,=\\,45), yet autoregressive GAR collapses for all variants (Figure~\\ref{fig:gate-shift}b).\n\n\\subsection{Mechanism Specificity}\\label{subsec:mechanism-specificity}\n\n\\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} showed that exposure bias selectively destroys GAR-ours (20--33$\\times$ gap) while, under standard training, GAR-Dongre remains unaffected. We test whether this selectivity extends beyond attention with an orthogonal hidden-state probe.\n\nWe define Turn-level Residual Coherence (TRC) as the cosine similarity between hidden states at the last-token position of adjacent assistant turns. If exposure bias caused broad representational failure, TRC should show a comparable gap. It does not. For ThreadGate, teacher-forced TRC is 0.334 and autoregressive TRC is 0.332 (gap $= +0.002$). For LoRA-only, teacher-forced TRC is 0.349 and autoregressive TRC is 0.411 (gap $= -0.062$, reversed): autoregressive generation does not degrade hidden-state coherence. Neither checkpoint shows a TRC gap comparable to the 20--33$\\times$ GAR collapse.\n\nThe slightly elevated autoregressive TRC for LoRA-only likely reflects higher token-level predictability in model-generated text, which produces more self-similar hidden states. These results confirm the selectivity from \\S\\ref{subsec:dist-shift} with an orthogonal metric: exposure bias disrupts a specific learned routing behavior (summary-position attention to goal tokens) while leaving internal representations intact.\n\nCross-model evaluation extends this selectivity: GAR-Dongre shows negligible distribution shift on both Llama-3.1-8B (AR/TF ratio 1.002$\\times$) and Qwen-2.5-7B (1.28$\\times$), compared to the 12.5--33$\\times$ collapse in GAR-ours, confirming that exposure bias primarily disrupts attention concentration at generation boundaries rather than aggregate attention patterns (the scheduled sampling experiment in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion} breaks this pattern, with GAR-Dongre rising to 0.353).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Model Generality}\\label{subsec:cross-model}\n\nTo test whether the exposure bias failure generalizes, we replicate the full evaluation pipeline on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct \\citep{grattafiori2024llama3}. Phase~1 diagnostics confirm goal attention decay (GAR: 0.026$\\to$0.003 over 10 turns, 90.1\\% decline vs.\\ Qwen's 84.7\\%). A matched evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, $T$\\,=\\,4) quantifies the exposure bias gap: teacher-forced GAR-ours is 0.484, autoregressive is 0.039, a 12.5$\\times$ gap directionally consistent with Qwen's 20--33$\\times$. The gate profile differs---Qwen's minimum at layers~3--4, Llama's at layer~25---yet both converge toward identity under NTP pressure. The exposure bias gap is consistent across architecturally distinct LLMs.",

    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013WdSatuS5U9pM1rgt3vms1`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Multi-turn dialogue with Large Language Models (LLMs) suffers from progressive attention decay toward system prompt goal tokens. On Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) declines by 70.8\% between early and mid-conversation turns, yet linear probes recover goal content from the residual stream with AUC\,=\,0.98. We introduce ThreadGate, a per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$) trained jointly with next-token prediction (NTP) and an auxiliary GAR loss. A five-way comparison reveals that training succeeds (teacher-forced GAR\,=\,0.50) but autoregressive GAR drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap; gating contributes zero measurable benefit and PCGrad-based causal exclusion rules out gradient conflict. The failure traces to exposure bias: attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text. Inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause. Scheduled sampling compresses the gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed), and cross-model evaluation on Llama-3.1-8B confirms the gap (12.5$\times$). These findings characterize how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary objective generalization under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses).
3	\end{abstract}
4

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MathXT1h4yk7sert28Avqq`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Multi-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.
4	
5	A striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \emph{not} affect. \citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\,=\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	  \centering
9	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
10	  \caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}
11	  \label{fig:overview}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	ThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\S\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way factorial comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one causal factor (\S\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and causal exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.
15	
16	Training succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad causal exclusion eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These four contrasts converge on a single root cause: exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the 32$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
17	
18	Our contributions: (1)~a systematic causal diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way factorial comparison that isolates four causal factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based causal exclusion via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification; this design pattern is in principle applicable to other auxiliary objective analyses (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from 32$\times$ to 1.57$\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (12.5$\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\beta$\,=\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \S\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011q2JeAAZBW2dFKdrgCvFtp`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}).
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the 32$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: steering trades fluency for goal recall; scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.
6	
7	\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\beta$\,=\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.
10	
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FhedkR91mV3eege2Juyfhr`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}).
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the 32$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: steering trades fluency for goal recall; scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.
6	
7	\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\beta$\,=\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.
10	
```

</details>

The two paths are complementary: steering trades fluency for goal recall; scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.\n\n\\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\\,=\\,0.200, $p$\\,=\\,0.007; McNemar $p$\\,=\\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.\n\nTRC analysis (\\S\\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \\citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \\citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.\n",

      {

          " ",
          " The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\\beta$\\,=\\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause (\\S\\ref{subsec:steering}).",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \\citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\\times$), compressing the 32$\\times$ gap to 1.57$\\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: steering trades fluency for goal recall; scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.",

GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: $\\beta$\\,=\\,5 achieves the strongest GAR recovery but incurs 24\\% PPL cost (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.77); $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (PPL +9\\%). Scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.",

[Director 指令] [Patch] Multi-seed 数据写入论文。Reviewer 已审定报告策略。

**1. v7 TG multi-seed（主要更新）**
两 seed 高度一致，论文从单 seed 升级为 mean±range：
- TF GAR-ours: 0.504±0.001（s42=0.5035, s123=0.5052）
- AR GAR-ours: 0.016±0.001（s42=0.0157, s123=0.0165）
- TF-AR gap: ~31×（s42=32.1×, s123=30.5×）
- PPL 无变化

影响章节：Abstract（gap 数字）、Experiments Table 1（如适用加 ±）、Discussion/Conclusion 中引用 gap 数字的地方。"32×" 如果是泛指 v7 gap，改为 "~31×" 或 ">30×"。

**2. v8b SS（supplementary observation）**
主结果保持 s42 数值不变（AR GAR 0.318, gap 1.57×）。在 Discussion 或 §6 加一段 training schedule observation：
> "A second seed trained from scratch (rather than resumed from a standard epoch) yields AR GAR=0.418 (gap 1.20×), while v7 TG shows only 5% seed variance under identical procedures. This suggests that early SS exposure—before the model consolidates TF-only attention patterns—may further reduce the distribution shift, consistent with the exposure bias hypothesis. Systematic schedule ablation is left to future work."

**3. 禁止事项**

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Multi-turn dialogue with Large Language Models (LLMs) suffers from progressive attention decay toward system prompt goal tokens. On Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR) declines by 70.8\% between early and mid-conversation turns, yet linear probes recover goal content from the residual stream with AUC\,=\,0.98. We introduce ThreadGate, a per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate ($x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$) trained jointly with next-token prediction (NTP) and an auxiliary GAR loss. A five-way comparison reveals that training succeeds (teacher-forced GAR\,=\,0.50) but autoregressive GAR drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap; gating contributes zero measurable benefit and PCGrad-based causal exclusion rules out gradient conflict. The failure traces to exposure bias: attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text. Inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause. Scheduled sampling compresses the gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed), and cross-model evaluation on Llama-3.1-8B confirms the gap (12.5$\times$). These findings characterize how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary objective generalization under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses).
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Limitations}\label{sec:limitations}
2	
3	Our experiments use a single random seed (seed\,=\,42). While the five-way convergence across ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, and LoRA-only provides indirect evidence of robustness (four independent configurations reaching indistinguishable performance is unlikely to result from seed-specific artifacts), multi-seed validation would strengthen this conclusion.
4	
5	We test only one type of auxiliary attention loss (GAR with MSE target $\tau = 0.5$). Other attention steering objectives, such as head-specific or layer-specific attention losses, may exhibit different sensitivity to exposure bias. Our negative result applies to the GAR family; it does not rule out all auxiliary attention interventions.
6	
7	Training uses 8K conversations from UltraChat. Larger-scale training could alter gate dynamics, particularly if the model encounters sufficient distributional diversity to reduce the training-inference mismatch.
8	
9	All experiments use two model families (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B); while cross-model evaluation confirms the exposure bias gap, $N$\,=\,2 does not establish universality. The Llama evaluation includes only the ThreadGate configuration; the full four-way ablation (LoRA-only, Uniform Gate) has not been replicated, and all experiments use a single dataset (UltraChat). The instruction retention analysis is exploratory: a single model, 18 system-prompt constraint types, and a single $\beta$ value without grid search.
10	
11	The PCGrad causal exclusion addresses gradient direction conflict only. Magnitude imbalance (GradNorm; \citealp{chen2018gradnorm}) and Nash equilibrium approaches (Nash-MTL; \citealp{navon2022nash}) remain untested and could reveal additional failure modes or interaction effects.
12	
13	All four fine-tuned variants share the same GAR auxiliary loss. This design isolates the gating mechanism but does not test whether gating could be effective without an auxiliary attention loss or under a different training objective.
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UYDVgywCsxGfz5WUHAUq3z`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Setup}
5	
6	We fine-tune Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct on 8K multi-turn conversations from the UltraChat training split, a dataset of naturalistic dialogues across diverse topics. LoRA adapters (rank 16, $\alpha$=32) are applied to query and value projection layers (\texttt{q\_proj}, \texttt{v\_proj}). The combined loss $\mathcal{L} = \mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \lambda_\text{GAR}\mathcal{L}_\text{GAR} + \lambda_\text{div}\mathcal{L}_\text{div}$ uses $\lambda_\text{GAR} = \lambda_\text{div} = 1.0$ with MSE target $\tau = 0.5$ (\S\ref{sec:loss}). We train for 3 epochs with AdamW (lr $= 2 \times 10^{-4}$, batch size 1, gradient accumulation 8, seed 42); each variant requires approximately 6 hours on a single NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition GPU (102\,GB). Full hyperparameters are provided in Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix-hyper}.
7	
8	We evaluate five configurations that form a factorial design isolating four causal factors. \textbf{ThreadGate}: full system with 28 gate networks (Eq.~\ref{eq:gate}) and three-term loss. \textbf{+PCGrad}: ThreadGate with PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} projecting conflicting gradients (isolates gradient conflict). \textbf{Uniform Gate}: gates without turn embedding (isolates turn conditioning). \textbf{LoRA-only}: gates fixed at 1.0, trained with $\mathcal{L}_\text{NTP} + \mathcal{L}_\text{GAR}$ only (isolates gating mechanism). \textbf{Base}: pretrained model (isolates fine-tuning). All fine-tuned variants share training data, LoRA configuration, optimizer, and seed. We evaluate under \emph{IID} (same-distribution UltraChat text) and \emph{held-out} (50 non-overlapping conversations) teacher-forced protocols, reporting GAR-ours (last-token attention to goals, \S\ref{sec:background}), GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention} (all-token average), and PPL.
9	
10	\subsection{Main Results}
11	
12	Table~\ref{tab:four-way} presents the five-way comparison. The central finding is that, under the single seed tested, all four fine-tuned variants converge to nearly identical performance. GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.
13	
14	The Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
15	
16	The teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. We analyze this distribution shift in \S\ref{sec:analysis}.
17	
18	\begin{table*}[t]
19	\centering
20	\small
21	\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. IID evaluation uses teacher-forced UltraChat text; held-out uses 50 non-overlapping conversations. All four fine-tuned variants converge to within 0.75\,pp on GAR-ours; PPL is indistinguishable. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \citep{dongre2026attention}.}
22	\label{tab:four-way}
23	\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}
24	\toprule
25	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{IID Evaluation}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{Held-out Evaluation}} \\
26	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
27	\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL \\
28	\midrule
29	Base              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 \\
30	ThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 \\
31	\;+\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
32	Uniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
33	LoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\
34	\bottomrule
35	\end{tabular}
36	\end{table*}
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	  \centering
40	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}
41	  \caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\,=\,45, $\pm$1\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\tau$\,=\,0.5 with 7--8\% decay (T1$\to$T4); Base decays 80\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}
42	  \label{fig:attention-heatmap}
43	\end{figure}
44	
45	\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\label{subsec:steering}
46	
47	The preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.
48	
49	Figure~\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\,=\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\beta \in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\,$\to$\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,5: GAR increases ${\sim}$11$\times$ (0.062\,$\to$\,0.6795$\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\% (1.41\,$\to$\,1.91). At $\beta$\,=\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.
50	
51	$N$\,=\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\,=\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\pm$0.110 ($\beta$\,=\,2) to 0.556$\pm$0.060 ($\beta$\,=\,3), an 8.7$\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\% PPL increase (1.43\,$\to$\,1.56), identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\beta$\,=\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\,=\,5 evaluation (0.028).
52	
53	These results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.
54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	  \centering
57	  \includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}
58	  \caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\pm$1\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\,=\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\beta \in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\sim}$11$\times$ recovery with 35\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\,=\,50, 39 with $\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\beta$\,=\,2 and $\beta$\,=\,3, identifying $\beta$\,=\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\times$ GAR recovery, 9\% PPL cost). High-$\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}
59	  \label{fig:steering}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62

GAR-ours spans only 0.75 percentage points (pp) across ThreadGate (0.50), +PCGrad (0.50), Uniform Gate (0.51), and LoRA-only (0.50), and PPL is 2.78 for all four on IID evaluation. Neither per-layer gating (ThreadGate), elimination of gradient direction conflict (+PCGrad), nor content-only gating (Uniform Gate) provides any measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.\n\nThe Base model confirms that LoRA fine-tuning is the operative variable: GAR-ours jumps from 0.01 to ${\\sim}$0.50, while PPL improves from 4.23 to 2.78. Since all four variants share the same GAR loss ($\\tau = 0.5$), convergence near the target is expected and does not demonstrate gating benefit. GAR-Dongre remains uniformly low (0.01--0.02) across all standard-trained conditions, consistent with \\citet{dongre2026attention}; a preliminary scheduled sampling experiment breaks this pattern (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\nThe teacher-forced GAR-ours of 0.50--0.51 stands in sharp contrast to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025 across all fine-tuned variants, a 20--33$\\times$ gap. This gap is universal: it persists across all training strategies---gating, gradient conflict elimination, and LoRA-only fine-tuning alike. We analyze this distribution shift in \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Five-way comparison on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct. IID evaluation uses teacher-forced UltraChat text; held-out uses 50 non-overlapping conversations. All four fine-tuned variants converge to within 0.75\\,pp on GAR-ours; PPL is indistinguishable. GAR-O = GAR-ours; GAR-D = GAR-Dongre \\citep{dongre2026attention}.}\n\\label{tab:four-way}\n\\begin{tabular}{l ccc ccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{IID Evaluation}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{Held-out Evaluation}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Configuration} & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL & GAR-O & GAR-D & PPL \\\\\n\\midrule\nBase              & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.23 & 0.01 & 0.00 & 4.34 \\\\\nThreadGate        & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.02 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\;+\\,PCGrad       & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nUniform Gate      & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.51 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\nLoRA-only         & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 & 0.50 & 0.01 & 2.78 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig4_attention_heatmap.pdf}\n  \\caption{Per-turn GAR-ours under teacher-forced evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,45, $\\pm$1\\,std). Fine-tuned variants maintain GAR near $\\tau$\\,=\\,0.5 with 7--8\\% decay (T1$\\to$T4); Base decays 80\\%. T5 std inflated by truncated conversations. Despite this preservation, autoregressive GAR collapses to 0.015--0.025 for all variants (Table~\\ref{tab:four-way}), distribution shift is the failure mode.}\n  \\label{fig:attention-heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Inference-Time Attention Steering}\\label{subsec:steering}\n\nThe preceding sections established that all training-time interventions fail uniformly: exposure bias collapses autoregressive GAR regardless of gating or gradient management. We now test whether inference-time attention manipulation can recover GAR without retraining. We apply an additive bias $\\beta$ to goal-token attention logits at every layer of the ThreadGate checkpoint during generation.\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:steering}(a) presents initial results on $N$\\,=\\,5 ten-turn conversations. Low bias values ($\\beta \\in [0, 2]$) yield negligible GAR change (0.028\\,$\\to$\\,0.062) with stable PPL (${\\sim}$1.4). A sharp nonlinearity appears between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,5: GAR increases ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ (0.062\\,$\\to$\\,0.6795$\\pm$0.020) while PPL rises only 35\\% (1.41\\,$\\to$\\,1.91). At $\\beta$\\,=\\,10, GAR saturates at 0.959 but PPL degrades to 4.22.\n\n$N$\\,=\\,50 conversations from a held-out evaluation set (39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns; Figure~\\ref{fig:steering}b) confirm the qualitative steering pattern observed in the $N$\\,=\\,5 pilot (10 turns each), with consistent nonlinearity range and saturation behavior. A finer $\\beta$ sweep localizes the nonlinearity: GAR jumps from 0.128$\\pm$0.110 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,2) to 0.556$\\pm$0.060 ($\\beta$\\,=\\,3), an 8.7$\\times$ recovery from baseline with only 9\\% PPL increase (1.43\\,$\\to$\\,1.56), identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point. The $\\beta$\\,=\\,0 distribution is right-skewed (mean 0.064, median 0.031, std 0.081), driven by short conversations with high baseline GAR; absolute baselines therefore differ from the 10-turn $N$\\,=\\,5 evaluation (0.028).\n\nThese results provide constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause of exposure-bias-induced GAR failure under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses): manually correcting attention via $\\beta$ bias restores GAR, and inference-time intervention circumvents the exposure bias barrier.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n  \\centering\n  \\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig5_attention_steering.pdf}\n  \\caption{Inference-time attention steering: additive bias $\\beta$ applied to goal-token attention logits during autoregressive generation (error bars show $\\pm$1\\,std). (a)~Initial evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,5, 10-turn conversations): sharp increase at $\\beta \\in [2, 5]$, GAR ${\\sim}$11$\\times$ recovery with 35\\% PPL rise. (b)~Larger-scale evaluation ($N$\\,=\\,50, 39 with $\\geq$3 turns, mean 3.6 user turns) with finer $\\beta$ sweep: the nonlinearity localizes between $\\beta$\\,=\\,2 and $\\beta$\\,=\\,3, identifying $\\beta$\\,=\\,3 as a practical operating point (8.7$\\times$ GAR recovery, 9\\% PPL cost). High-$\\beta$ results are consistent across sample sizes. Dotted line: unsteered teacher-forced baseline.}\n  \\label{fig:steering}\n\\end{figure}\n\n",
      "numLines": 62,

      "totalLines": 62
    }

1	\section{Discussion}\label{sec:discussion}
2	
3	The failure traces to an incompatibility between teacher-forcing and GAR-family auxiliary losses, not to the gating architecture. NTP gradient pressure (7.5--14.7$\times$ the GAR magnitude) pushes gates toward identity (2--7\% attenuation), while inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5) recovers GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause (\S\ref{subsec:steering}).
4	
5	\paragraph{Scheduled sampling as training-time mitigation.} A preliminary experiment (single seed) applies scheduled sampling \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, linearly mixing teacher-forced and model-generated contexts. Autoregressive GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the 32$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with negligible PPL change (1.38 vs.\ 1.36). GAR-Dongre also rises (0.019$\to$0.353), suggesting indirect improvement of all-token goal attention. The two paths are complementary: $\beta$\,=\,5 achieves the strongest GAR recovery but incurs 24\% PPL cost (1.43\,$\to$\,1.77); $\beta$\,=\,3 offers a more balanced tradeoff (PPL +9\%). Scheduled sampling incurs no fluency cost but requires retraining.
6	
7	\paragraph{Instruction retention under steering.} Across 18 system-prompt constraint types, $\beta$\,=\,3 steering increases content constraint compliance by +23.8\,pp (GAR-compliance $r$\,=\,0.200, $p$\,=\,0.007; McNemar $p$\,=\,0.077), providing exploratory evidence for downstream benefit pending multi-model confirmation.
8	
9	TRC analysis (\S\ref{subsec:mechanism-specificity}) confirms the mismatch targets attention routing without disrupting hidden-state coherence. We scope claims to GAR losses on Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B; magnitude imbalance \citep{chen2018gradnorm} and Nash equilibria \citep{navon2022nash} remain untested. Remaining directions include adaptive $\beta$ scheduling and layer-selective steering.
10

1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	ThreadGate modulates the residual stream with per-layer gated MLP networks and an auxiliary GAR loss. Training succeeds (TF GAR\,=\,0.50), but autoregressive GAR collapses 20--33$\times$ (exposure bias), confirmed on Llama-3.1-8B (12.5$\times$). Inference-time steering ($\beta$\,=\,5, GAR 0.727) and scheduled sampling (gap 32$\times$$\to$1.57$\times$, no PPL cost, single seed) both demonstrate attention misallocation---not representation degradation---as the proximal cause.

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01QThCrej7dgpxJvEuAveuzs`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Multi-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.
4	
5	A striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \emph{not} affect. \citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\,=\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention. This reasoning motivates ThreadGate, which modulates each residual update with a content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gate: $x_{l+1} = x_l + g_l \cdot f_l(x_l)$, where $g_l$ is produced by a two-layer network conditioned on the hidden state and turn features, inspired by the residual scaling idea of ReZero \citep{bachlechner2021rezero}.
6	
7	\begin{figure*}[t]
8	  \centering
9	  \includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig1_overview.pdf}
10	  \caption{Overview of ThreadGate and its failure mode. (Left) ThreadGate adds per-layer content-and-turn-conditioned MLP gates to the residual stream, trained jointly with an auxiliary GAR loss that targets goal attention. (Right) Under teacher-forced evaluation, GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants; under autoregressive generation, it drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap. The five-way comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) shows that no intervention provides measurable benefit over LoRA-only fine-tuning.}
11	  \label{fig:overview}
12	\end{figure*}
13	
14	ThreadGate combines per-layer MLP gate networks with a joint training objective: next-token prediction (NTP), an auxiliary GAR loss ($\tau = 0.5$), and a gate diversity loss (\S\ref{sec:method}). The final formulation emerged from seven iterations spanning four loss families; only MSE target with gate diversity converged stably. To diagnose the failure, we construct a five-way factorial comparison (ThreadGate, +PCGrad, Uniform Gate, LoRA-only, Base) where each pair isolates one causal factor (\S\ref{sec:experiments}). PCGrad \citep{yu2020pcgrad} serves a dual role: training strategy and causal exclusion tool that rules out gradient direction conflict.
15	
16	Training succeeds: teacher-forced GAR-ours reaches 0.50 across all fine-tuned variants. The factorial comparison reveals that the failure is specific to autoregressive generation, where GAR-ours drops to 0.015--0.025, a 20--33$\times$ gap that persists across all training strategies. All four fine-tuned variants achieve indistinguishable performance (0.75\,pp spread on GAR-ours, identical PPL at 2.78); the gating contrast confirms that gates contribute zero measurable benefit. The PCGrad causal exclusion eliminates gradient direction conflict without altering any outcome, establishing that conflict is neither necessary nor sufficient for the failure. These four contrasts converge on a single root cause: exposure bias \citep{bengio2015scheduled}, where attention patterns optimized under teacher-forced prefixes do not transfer to model-generated text (Figure~\ref{fig:overview}). An inference-time steering experiment provides constructive evidence that attention misallocation is the proximal cause: additive attention bias recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, bypassing the exposure bias barrier (\S\ref{subsec:steering}). A preliminary scheduled sampling experiment confirms that the gap is also mitigable at training time: GAR-ours increases from 0.016 to 0.318 (20$\times$), compressing the 32$\times$ gap to 1.57$\times$ with no PPL cost (single seed; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
17	
18	Our contributions: (1)~a systematic causal diagnostic framework for analyzing auxiliary objective failures in multi-turn dialogue, comprising a five-way factorial comparison that isolates four causal factors (turn conditioning, gating mechanism, gradient conflict, and fine-tuning), PCGrad-based causal exclusion via gradient surgery \citep{yu2020pcgrad}, and per-layer gradient conflict quantification; this design pattern is in principle applicable to other auxiliary objective analyses (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}); (2)~using this framework, the first empirical characterization of how exposure bias selectively destroys auxiliary attention loss generalization (20--33$\times$ gap) while leaving NTP robust, with evidence that token-level self-recovery \citep{he2019exposure} does not extend to auxiliary objectives, a preliminary demonstration that scheduled sampling compresses this gap from 32$\times$ to 1.57$\times$ (single seed), and cross-model confirmation on Llama-3.1-8B (12.5$\times$ gap); (3)~an inference-time steering experiment: additive attention bias at $\beta$\,=\,5 recovers autoregressive GAR from 0.064 to 0.727, providing constructive evidence that attention misallocation---not representation degradation---is the proximal cause under the tested conditions (Qwen-2.5-7B and Llama-3.1-8B, UltraChat, GAR-family losses; \S\ref{subsec:steering}); (4)~a principled negative baseline for future attention steering research: ThreadGate with seven loss formulations and four training configurations ruled out (\S\ref{sec:method}, \S\ref{sec:experiments}).
```

      "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn dialogue is the dominant mode of interaction with large language models (LLMs), and system prompts that specify task goals form the foundation of instruction following. As conversations grow longer, however, models progressively lose track of these goal specifications. \\citet{dongre2026attention} quantified this degradation across 12 models: the Goal Attention Ratio (GAR), which measures how much attention the model allocates to goal tokens in the system prompt, declines by 27--48\\% between early and mid-conversation turns. We replicate and extend this finding on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct, observing a 70.8\\% relative decline in GAR from 0.0418 at turn~1 to 0.0122 at mid-conversation. We attribute the steeper decline partly to our use of last-token GAR, which captures summary-position attention more sensitively than the all-token average of \\citet{dongre2026attention}, and partly to Qwen-2.5-7B's stronger instruction-following fidelity in early turns. This attention decay represents a systematic failure of multi-turn models to maintain task coherence, and it grows worse with each additional turn.\n\nA striking aspect of this degradation is what it does \\emph{not} affect. \\citet{dongre2026attention} showed that goal-relevant information persists in the residual stream throughout the conversation: linear discriminant analysis probes recover goal content with AUC\\,=\\,0.98 even at late turns where attention to goal tokens has collapsed. The information is present in the hidden states, but the attention mechanism fails to route it toward the output computation. This dissociation between information availability and information utilization suggests a natural intervention: if the residual stream already carries goal information, a learnable gate on the residual pathway could amplify or attenuate layer contributions to recover goal attention.
